Beth Mole – Nov 6, 2026 5:06 PM | 46 Boxes and vials of the Measles, Mumps, Rubella Virus Vaccine at a vaccine clinic put on by Lubbock Public Health Department on March 1, 2025 in Lubbock, Texas. Credit: Getty | Jan Sonnenmair Boxes and vials of the Measles, Mumps, Rubella Virus Vaccine at a vaccine clinic put on by Lubbock Public Health Department on March 1, 2025 in Lubbock, Texas. Credit: Getty | Jan Sonnenmair Text settings Story text Size Small Standard Large Width * Standard Wide Links Standard Orange * Subscribers only Learn more Minimize to nav An infant in Wisconsin has died of measles, the first measles death in the state since 1990, officials reported Thursday. The death comes amid an outbreak in the Badger State that has stricken over 430 people across 13 counties this year. “Any death of a child is a tragedy, and even more so when it is related to a vaccine-preventable disease,” Wisconsin Health Secretary Kirsten Johnson said in a statement. “Measles can cause serious illness and death, and it is important for all Wisconsinites to evaluate their risk and take steps to protect themselves and their family.” The baby’s death is the third measles fatality nationally just this week, following the death of a New York child reported on Monday and another death in Pennsylvania reported on Wednesday. The Pennsylvania death was of an unvaccinated person in Jefferson County; the state health department did not provide additional details, such as the age of the person. The state’s explosive, ongoing measles outbreak has led to 1,467 cases so far this year. In all, there have been nine reported measles deaths this year in the US, eight of which have been confirmed by state officials. The deaths include the baby’s death in Wisconsin, the child’s death in New York, and seven deaths (six confirmed) in Pennsylvania, including two babies, a healthy teen, and several adults with underlying health conditions. National context As of November 5, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention is reporting 5,182 measles cases in the US this year across 47 states and jurisdictions. The CDC is reporting four measles deaths, though it’s unclear which of the reported deaths the CDC has counted. Ars Video What Happens to the Developers When AI Can Code? | Ars Frontiers The national case and death tallies are the highest recorded since 1991, though the reported cases likely represent a significant undercount of actual cases. As Wisconsin health officials noted in the announcement of the baby’s death, “Not everyone who becomes ill seeks medical care or gets tested, which means cases may go unreported.” This is particularly true given that large outbreaks, including the one ongoing in Pennsylvania, have struck in tight-knit religious communities that are less likely to seek conventional medical care. In 2000, the US declared measles eliminated after a year without continuous domestic transmission, thanks to high vaccination rates. The declaration was a public health triumph over the virus, which is among the most contagious on Earth. Prior to vaccination, measles sickened 3 to 4 million people per year, sending nearly 50,000 to the hospital, causing 1,000 cases of brain swelling, and killing 400 to 500 people. Amid slipping vaccination rates nationwide and the spread of anti-vaccine misinformation and rhetoric, the virus has come roaring back, and the country’s elimination status is likely to be stripped. Next week (November 10 to 13), a committee of infectious disease experts assembled by the Pan American Health Organization—a regional office for the World Health Organization—will review data from 2025 and early 2026 to assess if measles has reestablished in the US. Politicized public health Under anti-vaccine Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., much about the vaccine-preventable outbreaks has become political and contentious, including the definition and confirmation of measles-associated deaths. Amid the first deaths reported in Pennsylvania, Kennedy falsely suggested state officials “fabricated” the deaths and reportedly ordered the CDC to delete them from its national data. The CDC then changed the way it counts measles deaths, abandoning a long-standing practice of reporting state-confirmed deaths. Now, the CDC relies on federal death records, which take much longer to process and are not intended to provide real-time data during active outbreaks. The seventh, unconfirmed Pennsylvania death was reportedly of a 39-year-old woman in Franklin County; it was announced on October 12 by the county’s Coroner’s office. At the time, the coroner explicitly said the death met the state’s definition of a measles-associated death. Under the state’s criteria, a death is considered measles-associated if the person dies within 30 days of testing positive and shows clinical signs of infection, with no other significant cause for the death, such as a car crash. Franklin County Coroner Jordan Conner listed the death as “a probable acute cardiac event due to possible complications of the measles” and reported that the woman had recently been diagnosed with and treated for measles. In an email exchange with Ars Technica on Thursday, the state health department said it “has not found any Franklin County cases” that meet the state’s criteria but that “the investigation has not yet been closed.” The Franklin County coroner did not respond to a request for comment on the discrepancy. The Philadelphia Inquirer has taken legal action to obtain more information about the woman’s death, including her name, which was withheld by the coroner and sealed by a county judge. This post was updated to correct the name of the Franklin County Coroner. Beth Mole Senior Health Reporter Beth Mole Senior Health Reporter Beth is Ars Technica’s Senior Health Reporter. Beth has a Ph.D. in microbiology from the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill and attended the Science Communication program at the University of California, Santa Cruz. She specializes in covering infectious diseases, public health, and microbes. 46 Comments Comments Forum view Loading comments... Prev story Next story Most Read 1. Wisconsin, United States; 430 cases across 13 counties; 1,467 cases statewide; 9 reported measles deaths in the US this year, 8 confirmed.
Event
Wisconsin officials reported the death of a baby from measles, the first such death in the state since 1990. This is part of an ongoing measles outbreak in the state and the US, with the baby's death being the third measles fatality nationally this week.
Scale, impact and risk
The outbreak has affected over 430 people in 13 counties in Wisconsin and 1,467 cases statewide. The baby's death is the third measles fatality nationally this week, with nine reported deaths in the US this year. The CDC is reporting 5,182 measles cases in the US this year, the highest since 1991, and four measles deaths, though it is unclear which deaths are counted.
Response, status and uncertainty
Wisconsin health officials noted that not everyone who becomes ill seeks medical care or gets tested, leading to underreporting. The CDC is monitoring the situation, and the national case and death tallies are the highest since 1991, though undercounting is likely. The US declared measles eliminated in 2000, but recent outbreaks suggest a resurgence.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Wisconsin infant dies of measles as state sees first death from disease since 1990

News Brief
An infant in Wisconsin has died of measles, marking the state's first measles-related death in over 36 years. This tragic event occurred amid a significant outbreak, with 431 cases reported across 13 counties, the highest number in more than 35 years. Nine individuals required hospitalization for severe symptoms, highlighting the severity of the outbreak. The Department of Health Services confirmed the cause of death as measles but chose not to release further details to protect the family's privacy. Officials have urged the public to take preventive measures, such as vaccination, to safeguard against the disease. Measles can lead to life-threatening complications, including pneumonia and brain swelling, particularly in vulnerable groups like young children, pregnant individuals, and those with weakened immune systems. The incident underscores the importance of maintaining high vaccination rates to prevent such outbreaks and protect public health.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

威斯康星州婴儿死亡 麻疹 暴发,自 1990 年以来首次死亡

Infant Dies in Wisconsin Measles Outbreak, First Death Since 1990
新闻简报An infant in Wisconsin has died from measles, marking the first fatality of the disease in the state since 1990. The Wisconsin Department of Health Services confirmed the death on November 6, 2026, during an ongoing outbreak that has seen the highest number of cases in over 35 years. The outbreak, which began in August 2026, has resulted in 431 confirmed cases across 13 counties, with nine individuals requiring hospitalization for severe symptoms. Officials stated that measles is a highly contagious respiratory virus that spreads through the air, and they emphasized the importance of the MMR vaccine, which is approximately 97 percent effective with two doses. However, they acknowledged that reported cases may not fully represent the extent of the outbreak, as not everyone who becomes ill seeks medical care or gets tested. The department also noted that about 95 percent of those infected were unvaccinated or had an undetermined vaccination status, highlighting the risk posed by low vaccination rates in the state.
查看新闻五要素
时间
威斯康星州卫生官员于 2026 年 11 月 6 日报告了这名婴儿的死亡,该事件是在自 2026 年 8 月 1 日起持续进行的 麻疹 暴发 期间发生的。
地点与对象
暴发 事件发生在威斯康星州西南部,影响人数不详,其中一名婴儿为确诊病例。
事件
一名婴儿因 麻疹 死亡,这是威斯康星州自 1990 年以来首次死于该疾病。威斯康星州卫生服务部证实了这起死亡事件,并表示死因是 麻疹。
规模、影响与风险
截至2026年11月6日,13个县共有431例麻疹病例,这是35年来的最高数字。九人因严重症状需要住院治疗。由于病例数和死亡人数较多,暴发 被认为是一个重大的公共卫生问题。
应对、状态与不确定性
Wisconsin health officials are actively investigating the outbreak and recommending that anyone exposed to measles stay at home. They also emphasize the importance of the MMR vaccine, noting that two doses are approximately 97 percent effective. However, they acknowledge that reported case counts may not reflect the full extent of the outbreak due to underreporting.

Open sidebar AD Wisconsin Infant Dies in Wisconsin Measles Outbreak, First Death Since 1990 State health officials announced the death amid the highest number of cases in more than 35 years. Save Mark Us Preferred on Google Print A sign providing information about measles in Seminole, Texas, on April 9, 2025. Brandon Bell/Getty Images Kimberly Hayek 11/6/2026|Updated: 11/6/2026 0:00 X 1 Wisconsin health officials reported on Thursday the death of an infant tied to the ongoing measles outbreak in the southwestern part of the state, making it the first fatality of its kind since 1990. The coroner declared the immediate cause of death as measles. To protect the privacy of the family and the child, the Wisconsin Department of Health Services will not release any additional information related to the death. “It is with a heavy heart we are reporting the first death of an infant connected with a measles outbreak in Wisconsin in many years. My heart is broken for the family,” the department’s secretary, Kirsten Johnson, said in a statement. “Any death of a child is a tragedy and even more so when it is related to a vaccine-preventable disease. Measles can cause serious illness and death, and it is important for all Wisconsinites to evaluate their risk and take steps to protect themselves and their family.” Measles is a highly contagious respiratory virus that spreads through the air when an infected person coughs or sneezes. Related Stories Pennsylvania Confirms 6th Measles-Associated Death New York Reports 1st Measles-Related Death in More Than 30 Years Symptoms typically begin with fever, cough, a runny nose, and red, watery eyes, followed several days later by a rash that starts on the face and spreads downward. The disease can lead to serious complications, including pneumonia and encephalitis. As of Nov. 3, the department has reported 431 measles cases in 13 counties so far in 2026, the highest number of cases seen in the state in more than 35 years. Across these cases, nine people have required hospitalization for severe symptoms. Reported case counts may not reflect the full extent of measles spread because not everyone who becomes ill seeks medical care or gets tested, which means cases may go unreported, according to the department. It recommends that anyone exposed to measles should stay at home. Wisconsin health officials said that the best way to prevent measles is with the measles, mumps, rubella (MMR) vaccine. Two doses are approximately 97 percent effective at preventing measles. Since Aug.1, more than 42,000 doses of MMR vaccine have been administered by healthcare providers in the state. About 95 percent of people who contracted the viral infection were unvaccinated or had an undetermined vaccination status, according to the department. Two percent had one dose of the MMR vaccine, while another 2 percent of cases had two doses of the vaccine. According to the most recent CDC data for the 2025–2026 school year, the lowest kindergarten MMR vaccination rates were recorded in Idaho (75.2 percent) and Wisconsin (85.1 percent), well below the national average of 92.4 percent. Other states with comparatively low coverage included Utah (87.2 percent), Arizona (87.4 percent), and Colorado (87.7 percent), while Alaska stood at 89.9 percent. High-coverage states such as West Virginia reached 98.9 percent. As of its Oct. 29 update, the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention listed 4,852 confirmed measles cases in the United States this year and four deaths. More than half of all cases were in children and young adults, the agency said. Approximately 17 percent were children younger than 5, and 42 percent were between ages 5 and 19. Eighteen measles cases were confirmed among international travelers to the United States. We had a problem loading this article. Please enable javascript or use a different browser. Wisconsin health officials reported the infant's death on November 6, 2026, during an ongoing measles outbreak that has been active since August 1, 2026.
Location and population
The outbreak occurred in the southwestern part of Wisconsin, affecting an unspecified number of people, with the infant being a confirmed case.
Event
An infant died due to measles, marking the first death from the disease in Wisconsin since 1990. The Wisconsin Department of Health Services confirmed the death and stated that the cause was measles.
Scale, impact and risk
As of November 6, 2026, there were 431 measles cases in 13 counties, the highest number in over 35 years. Nine individuals required hospitalization for severe symptoms. The outbreak is considered a significant public health concern due to the high number of cases and the fatality.
Response, status and uncertainty
Wisconsin health officials are actively investigating the outbreak and recommending that anyone exposed to measles stay at home. They also emphasize the importance of the MMR vaccine, noting that two doses are approximately 97 percent effective. However, they acknowledge that reported case counts may not reflect the full extent of the outbreak due to underreporting.

New York Had Its First Measles Death in 30 Years. Here’s What to Know - Smithsonian Magazine

News Brief
A 7-month-old child in New York died of measles on Monday, November 1, 2026, marking the state’s first measles death in over 30 years (N4). The incident occurred amid a surge in measles cases across the U.S., with more than 5,000 cases reported so far, and Pennsylvania experiencing the highest number of cases (N7, N8). The CDC notes that 96% of those infected this year were unvaccinated or had unknown vaccination status (N18). New York Governor Kathy Hochul called the death a 'devastating reminder' of the risks posed by declining vaccination rates and declared measles a statewide disaster last month (N5). The CDC has revised its immunization schedules, which may have contributed to the drop in MMR vaccination rates among kindergartners to 92.4%, below the 95% threshold needed for herd immunity (N13, N14). The child’s coroner confirmed they were not vaccinated (N19).

Skip to main content Search Shop Newsletters Renew Give a Gift Subscribe Membership i Subscribe Give a Gift Renew Sections Smart News History Science Innovation Arts & Culture Travel At the Smithsonian More from Smithsonian magazine Newsletters Photo Contest Podcast Videos Our Partners Smithsonian Store Smithsonian Journeys Subscribe to Smithsonian magazine and get a FREE tote. New York Had Its First Measles Death in 30 Years. Here’s What to Know Cases are still surging across the country amidst dropping vaccination rates Sara Hashemi | Daily Correspondent November 4, 2026 Share Copy linkEmailSMSFacebookXRedditLinkedInBlueskyPrint Add as preferred source Measles virus particle. Science Photo Library via Getty Images A 7-month-old child in New York died of measles on Monday, marking the state’s first death from the disease in more than 30 years. Report Ad New York Governor Kathy Hochul said in a statement that she was “heartbroken,” and that the death is “a devastating reminder of what’s at stake when Washington deliberately erodes decades of progress in protecting Americans from preventable illnesses.” Last month, Hochul declared measles a statewide disaster. Measles is a highly infectious viral illness that infects the respiratory tract. Once eliminated from the United States, cases in the country have now reached the highest they’ve been in 35 years, with more than 5,000 cases counted so far. Pennsylvania is seeing the worst of it, with 140 cases counted in just the last two weeks and more than a thousand this year. These outbreaks put the United States at risk of losing its official measles elimination status, gained in 2000. “The U.S. is in the throes of the deadliest measles outbreak it has seen in decades,” said Jennifer Nuzzo, an epidemiologist and director of the Pandemic Center at Brown University, to Lauren J. Young at Scientific American at the beginning of the year. “Losing measles elimination status is an official acknowledgement that the country is on the wrong path.” This comes at a time of increased vaccine hesitancy and widespread anti-vaccine rhetoric by officials like Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. In January, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) overhauled its immunization schedules for kids, dropping a number of recommended vaccines. While that decision didn’t include measles, vaccination rates for the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) combined shot in kindergartners have dropped to 92.4 percent, which is below the critical 95 percent threshold for herd immunity. Quick fact: Current immunization schedule for kids in the U.S. The CDC now recommends 11 immunizations for children, down from 17 in 2025. The six dropped from the list by January 2026 included immunizations for hepatitis A, hepatitis B, influenza, meningitis, respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) and rotavirus. Roughly 96 percent of people infected with measles this year were either unvaccinated or had an unknown vaccination status, according to CDC data. Jeffery Tompkins, the coroner and district attorney of Lewis County, where the child died, confirmed to Joseph Goldstein at the New York Times that the infant was not vaccinated. Children typically receive the first dose of the MMR vaccine when they are between 12 and 15 months of age, though public health authorities can recommend that a child receive a dose at 6 months if there is a community outbreak. “The MMR vaccine is the best way to prevent measles and stop outbreaks,” says Andrew D. Racine, the president of the American Academy of Pediatrics, to Melissa Jenco at AAP News. “It is safe, reliable, inexpensive and readily available. When enough people are vaccinated, the virus can’t spread throughout the community.” While most measles outbreaks still mainly happen in countries with low income or weak health infrastructure, the United States is not the only high-income country facing rising cases. Last year, the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) revoked Canada’s measles elimination status because it had 12 months of continual measles transmission. The United Kingdom is also experiencing a surge of cases. Experts blame decreasing vaccination rates for both countries. PAHO will meet from November 10 to 13, when the United States is widely expected to lose its measles elimination status. As of the time of the report, more than 5,000 measles cases have been counted in the United States, with the highest numbers in Pennsylvania (N7, N8). 美国 麻疹 病例自 1991 年以来首次突破 5,000 例,死亡人数达到 8 人 - astig.ph

US measles cases pass 5,000 for the first time since 1991 as deaths reach eight - astig.ph
新闻简报In 2026, the United States has seen a significant resurgence of measles, with 5,182 confirmed cases across 45 states and Washington, D.C., marking the highest number since 1991. This outbreak has resulted in eight deaths, including a child in New York and an infant in Wisconsin, the first measles-related fatalities in those states in decades. The CDC attributes the spread to declining vaccination rates, with only 92.4 percent of kindergarteners receiving their MMR shots in the 2025-2026 school year, down from 95.2 percent in 2019-2020. Most cases are among unvaccinated individuals, particularly in underimmunized rural communities. Pennsylvania has been the epicenter of the outbreak, with 1,222 cases and at least 219 hospitalizations. The CDC has confirmed four of the eight deaths, with the total likely to increase. In response, New York declared a state disaster emergency in October to accelerate testing and vaccination efforts. The outbreak has raised concerns about the U.S. potentially losing its measles elimination status since 2000. The CDC continues to monitor the situation and update figures weekly.
查看新闻五要素
时间
截至 2026 年 10 月 15 日星期五,CDC 更新了全国 麻疹 数据。暴发 于 2026 年 4 月在宾夕法尼亚州开始,一直持续到 2026 年 10 月。
地点与对象
美国,特别是 45 个州和华盛顿特区,重点是宾夕法尼亚州、纽约州和威斯康星州。受影响的人群包括未接种疫苗的个人,特别是在免疫不足的农村社区。
事件
CDC 确认 2026 年美国有 5,182 例 麻疹 病例,为 1991 年以来的最高水平,并报告有 8 人死亡,其中包括纽约的一名儿童和威斯康星州的一名婴儿。暴发 与低 疫苗接种 率以及 麻疹 在免疫覆盖率较差的社区中的传播有关。
规模、影响与风险
The outbreak has resulted in 5,182 confirmed cases and eight deaths, with 1,222 cases in Pennsylvania alone. The CDC noted that 2 percent of cases were among people who received one dose of the MMR vaccine, and another 2 percent received both recommended doses. The outbreak has raised concerns about the U.S. losing its measles elimination status since 2000.
应对、状态与不确定性
疾病预防控制中心每周更新其数据,迄今为止已确认八例死亡中的四例,但数字可能会发生变化。纽约州于 10 月宣布进入州灾难紧急状态,以加快测试和 疫苗接种 工作。暴发 正在进行中,CDC 将继续监测情况并做出反应。

Image: NIAID via Wikimedia Commons, CC BY 2.0 6 hours ago 6 hours ago LIVE US measles cases pass 5,000 for the first time since 1991 as deaths reach eight US measles cases have passed 5,000 for the first time since 1991, at 5,182 across 45 states and Washington, D.C., and eight people have died, including a child in New York and an infant in Wisconsin. #Measles #UnitedStates #PublicHealth #CDC by ASTIG PR 6 hours ago6 hours ago Advertisements Discover more Buy Maps Learn Tagalog Look Up Facts The United States has now confirmed more measles cases in 2026 than in any year since 1991, and the outbreak has killed eight people, including an infant in Wisconsin and a child in New York, the first measles deaths in those states in decades. CDC data updated on Friday put the national total at 5,182 confirmed cases, with infections reported in 45 states and Washington, D.C. Eighteen of the cases were in international visitors. What the latest CDC count shows It is the first time the national count has passed 5,000 since the early 1990s, when a resurgence from 1989 to 1991 produced 55,000 infections and 123 deaths. The 2026 tally is already more than double the 2,289 cases recorded in all of 2025. Most cases are in people who were not vaccinated. The CDC said 2 percent of cases were among people who had received one dose of the measles, mumps and rubella vaccine, and another 2 percent among people who had received both recommended doses. Measure Figure Confirmed US cases in 2026 5,182 States with reported cases 45, plus Washington, D.C. Measles-associated deaths in 2026 8 Deaths by state 6 in Pennsylvania, 1 in New York, 1 in Wisconsin Deaths confirmed by the CDC so far 4, a number the agency says is subject to change Cases in all of 2025 2,289 Kindergarten MMR coverage, 2025-2026 92.4 percent Kindergarten MMR coverage, 2019-2020 95.2 percent Two states recorded their first measles deaths in decades Discover more Compare Deals Launch a Store Compare Broadband New York’s health department said on Wednesday that a child who lived in upstate New York had died with measles, the first known measles-associated death in the state in more than 30 years. A day later, Wisconsin health officials said an infant had died with measles, the first measles death in that state since 1990. Six of the eight deaths have been in Pennsylvania. The state’s outbreak began in April west of Philadelphia and has centred on Lancaster County, which has sizable Amish and Mennonite populations. By October 14, Pennsylvania had confirmed 1,222 cases, its largest outbreak in more than three decades, with at least 219 people hospitalized. The CDC has confirmed four of the eight deaths nationwide and said the figure is subject to change. Why the outbreak keeps growing Vaccination coverage has slipped below the level needed to stop measles from spreading. About 92.4 percent of kindergarteners received their MMR shots in the 2025-2026 school year, down from 95.2 percent in 2019-2020, and only 10 states reached the 95 percent threshold last year. CBS News reported that the ongoing outbreaks have put the United States at risk of losing its measles elimination status, which it has held since 2000. Measles was declared eliminated that year through high vaccination rates, and coverage has since fallen under the roughly 95 percent needed to keep outbreaks from spreading. The CDC recommends two doses of the MMR vaccine, the first at 12 to 15 months and the second between 4 and 6 years old before school entry. One dose is about 93 percent effective, and two doses about 97 percent effective against measles. What New York did in October New York Governor Kathy Hochul declared a state disaster emergency in October as cases climbed in underimmunized rural communities, a step that let the state test and vaccinate people faster. New York had confirmed 108 measles cases as of October 3, including seven in New York City. Why the US count matters in the Philippines Measles spreads the same way wherever vaccination coverage falls. In Cebu province, this year’s measles-rubella immunization drive reached only 21.2 percent of its target children, down from about 90 percent in 2023, provincial health officials said, pointing to vaccine misinformation. That drive ran from August 10 to September 4 and targeted children aged six months to 59 months, with vitamin A supplementation. Coverage ranged from about 55 percent in the best-performing local government units to about 10 percent in the lowest. The CDC updates its national measles figures weekly. As of Friday, the 2026 count stood at 5,182 confirmed cases and eight deaths. (Image: A colorized transmission electron micrograph of a measles virus particle. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles cases on the rise in Minnesota with Todd County seeing by far the largest outbreak - Audacy

News Brief
A measles outbreak is currently occurring in Minnesota, with Todd County experiencing the largest number of cases. The Minnesota Department of Health reported 122 confirmed measles cases since mid-September, and Todd County has seen 115 total cases, including 58 confirmed in the last week. This outbreak is part of a broader resurgence of measles in the U.S., with Pennsylvania reporting over 1,350 cases this year, the highest in decades. Health officials have noted that an infant in Wisconsin and a child in New York have died from measles, marking the first deaths in those states since 1990. The outbreak is attributed to widespread vaccine hesitancy and declining vaccination rates, which have eroded herd immunity. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention reported that 92.4% of U.S. kindergarteners received the required MMR shots in 2025-26, down from 95.2% in 2019-20. The situation remains under investigation, with a focus on addressing vaccination gaps and preventing further spread.

He studied Journalism and Broadcasting at Anoka-Ramsey and Brown College before... See Full Bio Follow: Mark Freie Mark Freie See Full Bio Follow: Nov 06, 2026, 12:15 PM CT LocalNewswccoradio Measles cases continue to climb across Minnesota, echoing a pattern seen across a number of U.S. states. The Minnesota Department of Health report earlier this week that there are now 122 confirmed cases of measles since mid-September. Todd County has seen the biggest outbreak this year, with 115 total cases, including 58 confirmed cases just in the last week due to what health officials say is a widespread lack of vaccination. 139 total cases have been reported this year in total, compared to just 26 all of last year. This marks the highest total in Minnesota since 1990, and is nearly double the highest in the last decade. The news comes as health officials reported on Thursday that an infant has died from measles in southwestern Wisconsin, marking that state's first measles-related death since 1990. Other areas in the Northeast have been hard hit as well. Lancaster County in Pennsylvania has been the epicenter of the nation's worst measles outbreak in 35 years. It's also home to the largest Amish population in the U.S. Some Amish and related Mennonite groups, with historically lower rates of vaccination, have been hit hard. Vaccination rates also declined in the general population amid the resurgence of a contagious virus considered “eliminated” from the U.S. since 2000. Pennsylvania leads the U.S. this year with over 1,350 cases. A child in rural northern New York has also died after contracting measles, marking that state’s first death from the disease in 30 years. Measles spreads easily when people who have it breathe, cough or sneeze, or through contaminated surfaces. “Measles is really the lion king of infectious diseases when it comes to transmission,” said Michael Osterholm, who directs the University of Minnesota’s Center for Infectious Disease Research and Policy. “No one in the United States of America who is susceptible to the measles virus, either having not been vaccinated or not having previously had it, should feel safe from measles. No one.” The U.S. is currently experiencing a resurgence of measles, with the number of confirmed measles cases reported in the U.S. in both 2025 and 2026 exceeding that of any year since 1991. The risk grows as vaccination rates decline. Vaccine hesitancy that rose across populations during the pandemic still lingers. That's eroded “herd immunity,” which occurs when enough people in an area have immunity to measles that it no longer spreads easily. Although nothing short of 100% immunity guarantees the virus won’t spread, an outbreak is considered unlikely if a community has a 95% MMR rate. According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, 92.4% of U.S. kindergarteners in 2025-26 received the required MMR shots. That’s down from 95.2% in 2019-20. 克里斯蒂安县确诊 麻疹 病例 - KSMU

Measles case confirmed in Christian County - KSMU
新闻简报A measles case was confirmed in Christian County on November 5, 2026, following exposure to a case in another county. The Christian County Health Department reported that a resident tested positive, highlighting the need for vaccination to prevent outbreaks. Measles is highly contagious, with one infected person potentially spreading the virus to 9 out of 10 unvaccinated individuals. The health department is contacting those exposed and advising them to monitor for symptoms. They also emphasized that achieving community immunity through vaccination is crucial to prevent future outbreaks. While the current case is not expected to lead to a community outbreak, officials warned that without sufficient vaccination rates, future cases could pose a greater risk. The measles virus remains a public health concern, and the health department is urging residents to stay informed and follow preventive measures.

Over the Air & Streaming KSMU - Over the Air & Streaming Newsletters News Measles case confirmed in Christian County KSMU | By Michele Skalicky Published November 5, 2026 at 6:51 PM CST Facebook Twitter LinkedIn Email CDC The measles virus. The person infected was exposed to a confirmed case in another county. A case of measles has been reported to the Christian County Health Department. According to the department, a county resident tested positive after being exposed to a reported case in another county. Those who have been exposed are being contacted and asked to watch for symptoms. There have been three cases reported in Greene County this year. Christian County Health Administrator Aaron Winslow said in a statement that measles can no longer be viewed as "a disease of faraway places or the distant past." He said this case will not lead to a community outbreak due to efforts of state and public health officials, but he said they may not be so lucky next time. He said the best way to prevent an outbreak is to achieve community immunity through vaccination. "The best way to prevent getting sick is to be immunized with two doses of a measles-containing vaccine, which is primarily administered as the combination measles-mumps-rubella or MMR vaccine," according to the health department. "Two doses of the MMR vaccine provide 97 percent protection from measles infections." The kindergarten vaccination rate for MMR in Christian County for the 2025-2026 school year was 84.2%. Measles is a highly contagious viral illness – one person with the illness can easily pass it on to 9 out of 10 people around them who are unvaccinated or don’t have natural immunity. Symptoms start around two weeks from exposure and typically begin with fever, cough, runny nose and red and watery eyes. Tiny white spots may appear in the mouth two to three days after symptoms start. A rash begins three to five days after the first symptoms. Anyone who suspects they might have measles should call their healthcare provider. Measles can cause severe health complications, including pneumonia, encephalitis (inflammation of the brain) and death, according to the health department. The virus can be transmitted by direct contact with infectious droplets or by airborne spread when an infected person breathes, coughs or sneezes. The virus can remain infectious in the air and on surfaces for up to 2 hours after an infected person leaves an area. She enjoys working on both the announcing side and in news and has been the recipient of statewide and national awards for news reporting. She likes to tell stories that make a difference. Michele enjoys outdoor activities, including hiking, camping and leisurely kayaking. See stories by Michele Skalicky

The measles virus is the target entity, and related entities such as canine distemper virus, peste des petits ruminants virus, rinderpest virus, and cetacean morbillivirus are supplementary unless explicitly tied to the measles virus.

Response, status and uncertainty

一名婴儿死于 麻疹,这是威斯康星州自 1990 年以来首次死于该疾病 - PBS Wisconsin

时间
威斯康星州卫生服务部于 2026 年 11 月 5 日报告了婴儿的死亡情况。证据中没有明确说明事件日期,但死亡发生在该州的 麻疹 暴发 背景下。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Infant dies of measles in Wisconsin's first death from the disease since 1990 - PBS Wisconsin

News Brief
An infant in southwestern Wisconsin has died from measles, marking the state's first death from the disease since 1990. The Wisconsin Department of Health Services confirmed the cause of death as measles, though they did not specify the exact location to protect the family's privacy. This incident highlights the ongoing risk of measles due to declining vaccination rates, which have fallen below the 95% threshold needed to prevent outbreaks. In 2026, Wisconsin reported 431 measles cases, with nine hospitalizations, and all but three cases were linked to unvaccinated individuals. The U.S. has confirmed four measles-associated deaths in 2026, though state health departments report higher numbers. The event underscores the importance of maintaining high vaccination coverage to prevent the spread of vaccine-preventable diseases.

Associated Press November 5, 2026 • Southwest Region An undated electron microscope image provided by the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention on Feb. 4, 2015, shows a measles virus particle. (Source: Cynthia Goldsmith / CDC via AP, File) MADISON, Wis. (AP) — An infant in southwestern Wisconsin has died after contracting measles, the state’s first death from the disease in 36 years, state officials announced Nov. 5. The Wisconsin Department of Health Services did not specify where the death happened or provide any additional details to protect the privacy of the family. The coroner listed the cause of death as measles, state health officials said. “My heart is broken for the family,” said Kirsten Johnson, secretary of the state health department. “Any death of a child is a tragedy and even more so when it is related to a vaccine-preventable disease.” The U.S. stopped the domestic spread of measles in 2000 by maintaining high vaccination rates, but they have fallen in recent years to below the 95% coverage needed to prevent outbreaks of the viral disease. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention has confirmed four measles-associated deaths in the U.S. in 2026, but state health departments have reported higher numbers. In Pennsylvania alone, health officials have reported five deaths in 2026 and are investigating a possible sixth. As of Nov. 5, Wisconsin reported 431 cases of measles, all but three in unvaccinated people. Nine people had been hospitalized. ✓ Thanks for sharing! More... Notifications

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

随着历史悠久的 麻疹 暴发 登陆宾夕法尼亚州阿米什乡村,更多人对 疫苗 表现出开放态度 - The Well News

As Historic Measles Outbreak Hits Pennsylvania Amish Country, More Show Openness to Vaccines - The Well News
新闻简报历史性的 麻疹 暴发 袭击了宾夕法尼亚州阿米什县,今年该州报告了超过 1,350 起病例,仅兰开斯特县就有超过三分之一。暴发 促使阿米什和门诺派社区的一些成员对 疫苗 表现出开放态度,而其他人仍然持怀疑态度。自 2000 年以来,该病毒在美国被官方认为已“消灭”,但由于阿米什人中 疫苗接种 感染率较低以及普通公众感染率不断下降,该病毒正在传播。麻疹可引起肺炎、脑炎等严重并发症。卫生官员正在开展外展工作,包括设立流动诊所和小型 疫苗 诊所,以提高阿米什人和门诺派社区的 疫苗接种 发病率。这些努力已产生约 450 个 麻疹 镜头。该州的 麻疹 发病率较 9 月份的峰值有所下降,但一些医生预计,随着冬季的临近,麻疹 发病率会上升。暴发 强调了平原和更广泛的“英国”人口之间的相互联系,因为许多阿米什人和门诺派教徒定期通过购物和参加农贸市场等活动与公众互动。虽然大多数情况都很轻微,但 暴发 导致一些平原社区成员对 疫苗 的接受度激增,尽管其他人仍然由于文化和习惯原因而不是宗教教义而抵制。这种情况凸显了需要继续努力在这些社区建立信任并提高 疫苗接种 率。
查看新闻五要素
时间
我们使用 cookie 来收集有关您如何与我们网站互动的信息。
地点与对象
宾夕法尼亚州,特别是兰卡斯特县,该县是美国阿米什人口最多的地区,也是今年全国病例最多的州。
事件
历史性的 麻疹 暴发 袭击了宾夕法尼亚州阿米什县,今年该州报告了超过 1,350 起病例,仅兰开斯特县就有超过三分之一。暴发 促使阿米什和门诺派社区的一些成员对 疫苗 表现出开放态度,而其他人仍然持怀疑态度。
规模、影响与风险
麻疹 已导致兰卡斯特县发生 3 例与 麻疹 相关的死亡,其他县发生 2 例死亡。自 2000 年以来,该病毒在美国被官方认为已“消灭”,但由于阿米什人中 疫苗接种 感染率较低以及普通公众感染率不断下降,该病毒正在传播。麻疹可引起肺炎、脑炎等严重并发症。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生官员正在开展外展工作,包括设立流动诊所和小型 疫苗 诊所,以提高阿米什人和门诺派社区的 疫苗接种 发病率。这些努力已产生约 450 个 麻疹 镜头。该州的 麻疹 发病率较 9 月份的峰值有所下降,但一些医生预计,随着冬季的临近,麻疹 发病率会上升。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

As Historic Measles Outbreak Hits Pennsylvania Amish Country, More Show Openness to Vaccines - The Well News

News Brief
A historic measles outbreak has hit Pennsylvania Amish Country, with over 1,350 cases reported in the state this year, more than a third in Lancaster County alone. The outbreak has prompted some members of the Amish and Mennonite communities to show openness to vaccines, while others remain skeptical. The virus, officially considered 'eliminated' in the U.S. since 2000, is spreading due to low vaccination rates among the Amish and declining rates in the general public. Measles can cause serious complications such as pneumonia and encephalitis. Health officials are conducting outreach efforts, including mobile clinics and small-scale vaccine clinics, to increase vaccination rates among the Amish and Mennonite communities. These efforts have resulted in about 450 measles shots. The state has recorded a decline in measles rates from their September peak, but some doctors expect them to rise as winter approaches. The outbreak has underscored the interconnectedness of Plain and wider 'English' populations, as many Amish and Mennonites regularly interact with the general public through activities like shopping and attending farmers markets. While most cases are mild, the outbreak has led to a surge in vaccine acceptance among some Plain community members, though others still resist due to cultural and habitual reasons rather than religious doctrine. The situation highlights the need for continued efforts to build trust and improve vaccination rates in these communities.
Original source text (English)

Accept Decline HOME This Week 2026 New Members Elections Energy & Enviro Health Topics CongressEconomySupreme CourtWhite House Opinions Spotlight Donate Subscribe This Week 2026 New Members Elections Energy & Enviro Health Topics CongressEconomySupreme CourtWhite House Opinions Spotlight Donate Subscribe NEWS FROM THE WELL Subscribe to keep navigating our site Subscribe As Historic Measles Outbreak Hits Pennsylvania Amish Country, More Show Openness to Vaccines October 30, 2026by Peter Smith and Laura Ungar, Associated Press A member of the Plain community tends to horses at The Green Dragon Farmers Market and Auction in Ephrata, Pa., on Friday, Oct. 9, 2026. (AP Photo/Jessie Wardarski) EPHRATA, Pa. (AP) — Amish and Mennonite shoppers in conservative dress mingled with shoppers from the wider public on a recent Friday at a vast outdoor market here, where vendors sold colorful pumpkins and mums, fry pies and squash, sports jerseys and Barbie accessories. Such interactions are common in Lancaster County in central Pennsylvania, home to the largest Amish population in the U.S. — and the epicenter of the nation’s worst measles outbreak in 35 years. While they avoid many types of civic engagement for religious reasons, the Amish — like many other groups vulnerable to measles across the nation — are not truly separate. “We are all interconnected,” said Dr. Diego Hijano of St. Jude Children’s Research Hospital. Robust vaccination rates once protected Americans much better. But rates among the general public are now falling, and rates have long been low among the Amish, creating the perfect conditions for the contagious virus — officially considered “eliminated” from the U.S. since 2000 — to spread here and beyond. Pennsylvania has the most U.S. cases, with over 1,350 this year, more than a third in Lancaster County alone, according to state figures. That’s a big undercount, community leaders say, since many people with measles never see a doctor. The state has recorded three measles-associated deaths in Lancaster and two in other counties. In Amish Country, a Measles Surge Poses Questions About Vaccine Acceptance The outbreak has especially hit the “Plain communities” of Amish and conservative Mennonites — so named for their modest dress and limited use of technology. Vaccination rates, and attitudes about vaccines, range widely. But overall, they are less likely to be vaccinated than the general population, due more to culture and habit than doctrine. Many are now showing a new openness to vaccines, while members of the general public are also taking extra precautions. All of this has underscored the interconnectedness of Plain and wider “English” populations. The Plain People avoid such areas of civic participation as holding public office. Still, they regularly shop and do business with their neighbors in this growing county of half a million people, where an urban core is ringed by suburban subdivisions abutting Amish farms. “There’s no way to imagine that this is going to be an isolated community,” said Dr. Anne-Marie Derrico, chief medical officer at the nonprofit Union Community Care. It has clinics in the city of Lancaster and outlying towns, along with a mobile unit operating a “Plain Healthy” outreach to the Amish and to Mennonites. “We have Plain patients coming to the city, we have patients in the city who don’t vaccinate, who are going to school, going to other programs with other children,” Derrico said. Pennsylvania’s measles rates have declined from their September peak, but some doctors expect them to rise as winter brings more indoor gatherings. While most cases are mild, measles can cause serious and even fatal complications, such as pneumonia and encephalitis. Union has held small-scale vaccine clinics to reach Plain populations, resulting in about 450 measles shots. “That’s hopefully made a significant impact,” Derrico said. Union’s clinics are also offering vaccinations for infants earlier than normally scheduled, as health experts advise in areas experiencing measles outbreaks. Lauren and Mason Spence brought their 10-month-old son, Bowen, to a clinic recently to get his first dose of the MMR vaccine, short for measles, mumps and rubella. The “English” family frequently goes to public places, including farmers markets. “It’s scary,” Lauren Spence said. “It’s spreading quick.” Vaccine Views Vary Among Plain People Attitudes toward vaccines in Plain communities have alternately warmed and cooled over time — most recently cooling in reaction to strict COVID-19 public health measures, according to community leaders and scholars. The measles outbreak is prompting many to accept the shots while reinforcing vaccine skepticism in others. One Amish farmer, Ephraim Zook, still opposes vaccines even after he and his wife nursed their young twins through the illness. “We just felt that measles is a natural thing,” he said. A Mennonite market vendor said his largely vaccinated community has had no infections. One Plain farmer said he got vaccinated after his adult siblings began getting measles. With vegetables to harvest and tobacco leaves to pick, “I thought, if I get it, I’m going to be done,” he said. Most interviewed by the AP asked not to use their names, in keeping with Plain traditions of not calling attention to themselves. Those who avoid vaccines usually aren’t driven by ideology, community leaders and experts say. “The overwhelming reason had nothing to do with being against vaccinations,” said Allen Hoover, an Old Order Mennonite and former administrator of the Parochial Medical Center in the town of New Holland. “It was simply, ‘Well, we never did before, and we came through all right.’” Hoover said the most effective strategy for medical workers is to encourage vaccination — not to push it aggressively. It takes time for outside professionals to build trust in Plain communities, he said. About how long? “Twenty years,” he said. One cluster of Amish schools and individuals in New York is asking the U.S. Supreme Court to require their state to allow exemptions to school vaccine requirements on religious grounds, as Pennsylvania and most states do. But the wider Amish leadership hasn’t presented this as a religious issue, according to Steve Nolt, director of the Young Center for Anabaptist and Pietist Studies at Elizabethtown College in Lancaster County. “There’s nothing in Amish theology or historic documents that you can find that specifically addresses or is opposed to immunization,” he said. About 45,000 Amish and 6,000 Old Order Mennonites live in and around Lancaster County, according to the Young Center. Many rely on traditional remedies and visit doctors infrequently, Nolt said. This year’s outbreak follows ones in 2025 in Mennonite communities in Texas and elsewhere across North America. Contagious Virus a Rising Threat to Everyone Measles spreads easily when people who have it breathe, cough or sneeze, or through contaminated surfaces. “Measles is really the lion king of infectious diseases when it comes to transmission,” said Michael Osterholm, who directs the University of Minnesota’s Center for Infectious Disease Research and Policy. “No one in the United States of America who is susceptible to the measles virus, either having not been vaccinated or not having previously had it, should feel safe from measles. No one.” The risk grows as vaccination rates decline. According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, 92.4% of U.S. kindergarteners in 2025-26 received the required MMR shots. That’s down from 95.2% in 2019-20. “Though Plain communities are an easy and recognizable target, declining childhood vaccination rates in our county are not limited to the Amish,” wrote Berwood Yost, director of the Center for Opinion Research at Franklin & Marshall College in Lancaster, in a recent commentary. “Kindergarten vaccination rates have declined in every central Pennsylvania county since 2020.” A 2022 survey by the center found the vast majority of Mennonite families reported having their children vaccinated, compared to about 3 in 10 Amish families. Yost urged the public not to blame the Amish, or any group, for the outbreak. “Every one of us is susceptible to misinformation and confusing messages,” he wrote. Vaccine hesitancy that rose across populations during the pandemic still lingers. That’s eroded “herd immunity,” which occurs when enough people in an area have immunity to measles that it no longer spreads easily. Although nothing short of 100% immunity guarantees the virus won’t spread, an outbreak is considered unlikely if a community has a 95% MMR rate. Pennsylvania’s kindergarten MMR vaccination rate fell to 93.2% last year. Lancaster County’s rate is 88%, with rates lower in the county’s religious and other private schools than in public schools, according to an AP review of state data. Across the county and state, the percentage of students receiving religious or philosophical exemptions from at least one required vaccination has risen significantly. Nolt said some anti-vaccine groups targeted the Amish with their messaging. In 2021, Robert F. Kennedy Jr., who helped lead the anti-vaccine movement for years, spoke at a Lancaster County fair, alleging the government was trying to force the Amish to vaccinate. Now U.S. health secretary, Kennedy recently sparred with Pennsylvania’s Democratic governor, Josh Shapiro, over the extent of the outbreak. Further controversy ensued when the county coroner attributed the death of a measles-infected infant to a ruptured spleen, not the disease itself. After a Decline in Trust, Some Minds Are Changing Lancaster County Commissioner Alice Yoder said public officials should unite behind the message that the vaccine is safe and effective and should encourage people to get it. That approach succeeded during Pennsylvania’s last measles outbreak in 1991. “There were lines of people in the Plain community that would come and get vaccinated,” she said. Dr. D. Holmes Morton, who founded the Central Pennsylvania Clinic in Mifflin County — which treats Plain patients with genetic diseases — said the same is happening now. “We are having more success at having vaccine days,” he said. Ultimately, this will keep everyone safer, said Hijano of St. Jude. Getting the shot is “an act of love to your community,” he said. “You will get protected yourself. But in truth, you are actually protecting everyone around you.” ___ Associated Press religion coverage receives support through the AP’s collaboration with The Conversation US, with funding from Lilly Endowment Inc. B routine priorityPublic-health news

What parents need to know as measles outbreaks spread across the US - Good Morning America

News Brief
As measles outbreaks spread across the U.S., health officials report nearly 5,200 cases across 45 states and Washington, D.C., with eight deaths, including an infant in Wisconsin, marking the first measles-related death in the state since 1990. The measles virus is highly contagious, spreading through the air and potentially infecting up to 18 unvaccinated individuals per case. Symptoms include a rash, cough, fever, and Koplik spots, which can appear before the rash. Serious complications, such as severe pneumonias, meningitis, and even death, are possible. Dr. Andrew Handel, a pediatric infectious diseases physician, highlights the importance of the MMR vaccine in preventing infections, noting it is safe and effective. However, some groups, like those with compromised immune systems or pregnant women, may not be eligible for vaccination. The CDC urges parents to consult medical providers regarding their children's and family's eligibility and concerns.
Original source text (English)
Wellness What parents need to know as measles outbreaks spread across the US 1:03 New measles death in Wisconsin New measles death in Wisconsin By Yi-Jin Yu November 06, 2026, 10:24 PM More measles cases are being reported across the U.S., and eight people have died so far, including an infant in Wisconsin , the first measles-related death in the state since 1990, according to health officials. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention says there have been nearly 5,200 measles cases across 45 states and Washington, D.C., so far this year, as more have decided to opt out of vaccination. "I'm extremely concerned, and the reason for that is there's no indication that these outbreaks will be slowing down at this point," Dr. Andrew Handel, a pediatric infectious diseases physician at Stony Brook Children's Hospital in Stony Brook, New York, told ABC News. As the measles virus continues to spread, here's what parents should know about the disease. What is measles? Measles is a highly contagious virus that can cause acute respiratory illness, according to the CDC . It is more contagious that other respiratory viruses, such as the common cold, the flu or COVID. "It is the most contagious infection that we know of," Handel said. "The estimate is that for every person who has measles, they can infect up to 18 people who are unvaccinated with it." The measles virus in an image from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention website. CDC Measles is spread through the air when someone infected with the virus exhales or coughs. People can also become infected after breathing in contaminated air or by touching an infected surface and then touching their eyes, noses or mouths, the CDC states. "For measles, it spreads through airborne means, which means that once [an infected person breathes] it out, it actually stays circulating in the air for up to two hours after they leave that room," Handel explained. "If someone goes to their doctor to be checked for measles, and they don't recognize that they have measles, and then they walk out of the room, someone who comes in an hour and a half later can become infected with measles." What are the symptoms of measles? The CDC notes that measles can initially look like other respiratory infections, with symptoms including a cough, runny nose, conjunctivitis (red, watery eyes), or a high fever, which can increase to more than 104 degrees Fahrenheit. The most recognizable symptom of measles is a rash that typically starts at the head and moves down the body. "People break out into the rash, which starts as red, dark circles that usually start right at the hairline and work their way down the body," Handel said. "As it works its way down, you see the red circles, the red dots further down. But the place where it started -- so, on the face -- the redness will start to come together, so it'll look like a sheet of redness, and it, again, starts from the head and works its way down, which is very particular." Related Articles Measles cases surpass 5,000 in the US with infections confirmed in 45 states: CDC "We also see something called Koplik spots, which are usually whitish, bluish dots that can appear on the inside of the cheek," Handel added. "Those tend to come up a couple days before the rash, so it can help us to make the diagnosis a bit earlier." Measles symptoms typically begin seven to 14 days after initial contact with the virus, according to the CDC. What are the complications of measles? A common misconception about measles, as the CDC notes, is that infection with the virus isn't severe. The CDC and Handel both stress that measles infections can give rise to serious complications , from short-term issues like diarrhea and ear infections, to serious to life-threatening long-term problems and death. "Many children, many people can go on and have complications ... that can include severe pneumonias, dehydration, and also acute brain infections, where suddenly, you can have seizures," Handel said. He continued, "You can have changes in behavior. You can develop meningitis where there's neck pain and headache because the measles infection [has] infiltrated into the spinal fluid. There are some children who, especially if they're infected before they turn 1 year old, in particular, can develop a neurologic progressive disease [called subacute sclerosing panencephalitis, or SSPE,] that ultimately results in death up to 10 years later. And so, there are some really scary outcomes from it." Related Articles As measles continues to spread, health officials are seeing vaccine interest rise How can measles be prevented? According to the CDC, the best way to protect against measles is with the measles, mumps, and rubella vaccine , or MMR, which can be administered to children starting at 12 months old. Children who receive a first dose of the MMR vaccine then need to get a second dose when they are between 4 and 6 years old. "The only way to prevent infections when there is an ongoing national outbreak at levels that we haven't seen in decades is to get the MMR vaccine," Handel said. "It is safe, it is effective, and it's the best thing we can do again to stop this outbreak and get the country back to the rates of measles infections that we'd previously seen." Not everyone may be able to get an MMR vaccine, such as children with cancer, those who are pregnant, and people who have compromised immune systems, so it is important to discuss your children's and family's eligibility and concerns with a medical provider. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

CDC:美国 麻疹 病例超过 5,000 例,45 个州确诊感染

Measles cases surpass 5,000 in the US with infections confirmed in 45 states: CDC
新闻简报As of November 6, 2026, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) reported that measles cases in the United States have surpassed 5,000 this year, with infections confirmed in 45 states and Washington, D.C. Eight measles-associated deaths have been confirmed, with the CDC noting that four of these deaths have been verified, and the remaining four are subject to change. The majority of cases, 96%, are among individuals who are unvaccinated or have an unknown vaccination status. Additionally, 11% of cases have resulted in hospitalization, including more than 1 in 10 children under 5 years old. The CDC recommends two doses of the measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine, but vaccination rates have declined in recent years, particularly during the 2025-26 school year, when 92.4% of kindergartners received the vaccine, compared to 95.2% in the 2019-20 school year. The outbreak highlights the importance of maintaining high vaccination rates to prevent the spread of measles.
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026-11-05,事件日期范围从2025-26学年到2026年11月6日。
地点与对象
美国 45 个州和华盛顿特区,在未接种疫苗的个体和 疫苗接种 状态未知的个体中出现病例。
事件
据 CDC 报道,今年美国的 麻疹 病例已超过 5,000 例,其中确认了 8 例与 麻疹 相关的死亡。
规模、影响与风险
至少 5,182 例确诊病例,11% 住院治疗,8 人死亡。CDC 指出,96% 的病例是未接种疫苗或身份不明的个体。
应对、状态与不确定性
疾病预防控制中心已确认八例死亡中的四例,但数字可能会发生变化。CDC 建议接种两剂 MMR 疫苗,但 疫苗接种 的发病率近年来一直滞后。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles cases surpass 5,000 in the US with infections confirmed in 45 states: CDC

News Brief
As of November 6, 2026, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) reported that measles cases in the United States have surpassed 5,000 this year, with infections confirmed in 45 states and Washington, D.C. Eight measles-associated deaths have been confirmed, with the CDC noting that four of these deaths have been verified, and the remaining four are subject to change. The majority of cases, 96%, are among individuals who are unvaccinated or have an unknown vaccination status. Additionally, 11% of cases have resulted in hospitalization, including more than 1 in 10 children under 5 years old. The CDC recommends two doses of the measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine, but vaccination rates have declined in recent years, particularly during the 2025-26 school year, when 92.4% of kindergartners received the vaccine, compared to 95.2% in the 2019-20 school year. The outbreak highlights the importance of maintaining high vaccination rates to prevent the spread of measles.
Original source text (English)

OffOn Stream on Measles cases surpass 5,000 in the US with infections confirmed in 45 states: CDC Eight people have died from measles this year. ByMary Kekatos November 6, 2026, 10:13 AM Share Follow ABC News on Google Measles cases in the United States have surpassed 5,000 this year, according to newly updated data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). At least 5,182 cases have been confirmed, with cases reported in 45 states and Washington, D.C. Eighteen cases have been among international visitors to the U.S., according to the CDC. Eight measles-associated deaths have been confirmed so far in 2026: six in Pennsylvania, one in New York and one in Wisconsin. Related New map shows how to spot the measles risk level in your ZIP code Three of those deaths were confirmed by state health officials this week. On Wednesday, the New York State Department of Health reported the first known measles-associated death in the state in more than 30 years, a child who lived in upstate New York. Another death was confirmed on Wednesday in Pennsylvania among an unvaccinated resident of Jefferson County, located in the northwestern part of the state. On Thursday, Wisconsin health officials said an infant died with measles, the first death in the state since 1990. Undated electron microscope image provided by the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, shows a measles virus particle. Cynthia Goldsmith/Centers for Disease Control and Prevention via AP The CDC has confirmed four of the eight deaths so far, but said the number is "subject to change." Popular Reads Thomas Markle Sr. arrested, charged with possession of child pornography 9 hours ago 4 Colorado officers shot, 1 fatally, after gunfight with suspect: Police 10 hours ago US attorney tasked with leading probe into Trump's foes resigns from DOJ 9 hours ago States with confirmed cases include Alabama, Alaska, Arizona, California, Colorado, Connecticut, Delaware, Florida, Georgia, Idaho, Illinois, Indiana, Iowa, Kansas, Kentucky, Louisiana, Maine, Maryland, Massachusetts, Michigan, Minnesota, Missouri, Montana, Nebraska, New Jersey, New Mexico, New York, North Carolina, North Dakota, Ohio, Oklahoma, Oregon, Pennsylvania, Rhode Island, South Carolina, South Dakota, Tennessee, Texas, Utah, Vermont, Virginia, Washington, West Virginia, Wisconsin and Wyoming. About 96% of cases are among people who are unvaccinated or whose vaccination status is unknown, according to data from the federal health agency. Meanwhile, 2% of cases are among those who have received just one dose of the measles, mumps, rubella (MMR) vaccine and 2% of cases are among those who received the recommended two doses, according to the CDC. Related How hospitals, clinics in Pennsylvania are responding to the measles outbreak A plurality of cases has been recorded among those aged 5 to 19. At least 11% of cases have resulted in hospitalization, including more than 1 in 10 children under 5 years old. The CDC currently recommends that people receive two doses of the MMR vaccine, the first at ages 12 to 15 months and the second between 4 and 6 years old before school entry. One dose is 93% effective, and two doses are 97% effective against measles, the CDC says. However, CDC data shows vaccination rates have been lagging in recent years. During the 2025-26 school year, 92.4% of kindergartners received the MMR vaccine, according to data. This is lower than the 95.2% seen in the 2019-20 school year, prior to the COVID-19 pandemic. ABC News' Youri Benadjaoud contributed to this report. Response, status and uncertainty
